Ayurveda Knowledge Base — Part 1: Foundational Theory
What is Ayurveda

Ayurveda ("science of life" — ayur = life, veda = knowledge) is a traditional Indian system of medicine, over 3,000 years old, rooted primarily in two classical texts: the Charaka Samhita (internal medicine) and the Sushruta Samhita (surgery). A later compilation, the Ashtanga Hridaya by Vagbhata, synthesizes both. Ayurveda treats health as a dynamic balance between mind, body, and environment, rather than the absence of disease alone.

The Panchamahabhuta (Five Great Elements)

Ayurveda holds that everything in the universe, including the human body, is composed of five elements:

Akasha (space/ether)
Vayu (air)
Agni (fire)
Jala (water)
Prithvi (earth)

These combine in pairs to form the three doshas.

The Tridosha (Three Doshas)

The doshas are functional bio-energies said to govern all physiological and psychological processes. Every person has a unique proportion of all three, called their Prakriti (constitution).

Vata Dosha (Space + Air)
Governs movement: nerve impulses, circulation, breathing, elimination, joint movement.
Qualities: dry, light, cold, rough, subtle, mobile, clear.
Seat: colon, thighs, bones, ears, skin.
When balanced: creativity, flexibility, quick thinking.
When aggravated: anxiety, insomnia, constipation, dry skin, joint pain, irregular digestion.
Pitta Dosha (Fire + Water)
Governs transformation: digestion, metabolism, body temperature, comprehension.
Qualities: hot, sharp, light, oily, liquid, spreading.
Seat: small intestine, stomach, sweat glands, blood, eyes.
When balanced: sharp intellect, strong digestion, good complexion.
When aggravated: inflammation, acidity, anger, skin rashes, excessive body heat.
Kapha Dosha (Water + Earth)
Governs structure and stability: bodily fluids, immunity, lubrication of joints, tissue growth.
Qualities: heavy, slow, cold, oily, smooth, dense, stable.
Seat: chest, lungs, throat, head, joints, plasma.
When balanced: strength, stamina, calmness, immunity.
When aggravated: congestion, weight gain, lethargy, attachment, water retention.
Dhatus (Seven Bodily Tissues)

The body's structural tissues, formed in sequence, each nourishing the next:

Rasa — plasma/lymph
Rakta — blood
Mamsa — muscle
Meda — adipose/fat
Asthi — bone
Majja — bone marrow/nerve tissue
Shukra — reproductive tissue
Malas (Waste Products)

Ayurveda identifies three primary excretory products whose proper elimination is considered essential to health:

Purisha (feces)
Mutra (urine)
Sweda (sweat)
Agni (Digestive Fire)

Agni refers to the body's transformative/metabolic capacity, most prominently digestive fire (Jatharagni) but also present at the tissue level (Dhatvagni) and cellular level (Bhutagni). Four states of Agni are described:

Sama Agni — balanced, optimal digestion
Vishama Agni — irregular (associated with Vata imbalance)
Tikshna Agni — excessively sharp (associated with Pitta imbalance)
Manda Agni — slow/weak (associated with Kapha imbalance)
Ama (Toxic Residue)

When Agni is impaired, undigested/unmetabolized material called Ama accumulates. Ama is considered a root cause of disease in Ayurvedic theory, described as a sticky, toxic byproduct that obstructs channels (Srotas) and vitiates doshas.

Srotas (Channels)

Physiological/anatomical channels through which substances (nutrients, waste, doshas) circulate. Classical texts describe 13+ major Srotas, including channels for respiration, digestion, circulation, water metabolism, and reproduction. Blockage or damage to Srotas is a common explanatory model for disease.

Prakriti (Constitution) and Vikriti (Current State)
Prakriti is the individual's inborn dosha ratio, fixed at conception and considered a lifelong baseline.
Vikriti is the current, possibly imbalanced state of the doshas, influenced by diet, lifestyle, season, stress, and age.
Treatment in Ayurveda is generally aimed at bringing Vikriti back in line with a person's Prakriti, not at forcing everyone toward one "ideal" dosha ratio.
Guna (Qualities) and the Ten Pairs of Opposites

Ayurveda describes matter and states of the body/mind using paired qualities (heavy–light, cold–hot, dull–sharp, stable–mobile, soft–hard, non-slimy–slimy/oily, smooth–coarse, minute–gross, viscous–liquid/flowing, static–mobile). Treatment principles often work by applying the opposite quality to correct an imbalance (e.g., a cold, oily therapy to pacify a hot, dry Pitta condition).

Ojas

Considered the subtle essence of all seven dhatus, Ojas is associated with vitality, immunity, and resilience. Depletion of Ojas (through stress, poor digestion, overexertion) is linked in classical theory to susceptibility to disease.

Manas (Mind) and the Three Gunas of Mind

Beyond the physical doshas, Ayurveda describes three qualities governing mental/psychological states:

Sattva — clarity, harmony, balance
Rajas — activity, passion, restlessness
Tamas — inertia, dullness, resistance to change


# Ayurveda Knowledge Base — Part 2: Diagnosis and Treatment Systems

## Diagnostic Methods

### Trividha Pariksha (Three-fold Examination)
The classical minimum diagnostic framework:
1. **Darshana** — observation (skin, tongue, eyes, posture, gait, complexion)
2. **Sparshana** — palpation/touch (pulse, skin temperature, texture, tenderness)
3. **Prashna** — interrogation (history-taking: symptoms, diet, lifestyle, onset)

### Ashtavidha Pariksha (Eight-fold Examination)
An expanded diagnostic checklist:
1. **Nadi** (pulse)
2. **Mutra** (urine)
3. **Mala** (stool)
4. **Jihva** (tongue)
5. **Shabda** (voice/speech)
6. **Sparsha** (touch/skin)
7. **Drik** (eyes)
8. **Akriti** (general body form/build)

### Nadi Pariksha (Pulse Diagnosis)
A signature Ayurvedic diagnostic technique in which the physician reads the radial pulse with three fingers (index, middle, ring), each said to correspond to Vata, Pitta, and Kapha respectively. Pulse qualities (rate, depth, rhythm, strength) are interpreted to assess dosha imbalance. This is a skill-based clinical technique, not a lab test, and its interpretation varies by practitioner training.

### Prakriti Assessment
Determining a person's constitutional type (Vata, Pitta, Kapha, or combinations) via body frame, skin/hair qualities, digestion pattern, sleep pattern, temperament, and pulse — used to individualize diet, lifestyle, and treatment recommendations.

## Nidana Panchaka (Five-fold Diagnosis of Disease)
Classical disease diagnosis considers five components:
1. **Nidana** — causative factors
2. **Purvarupa** — prodromal symptoms
3. **Rupa** — the fully manifested signs/symptoms
4. **Upashaya** — response to trial therapies (what relieves or worsens the condition)
5. **Samprapti** — the pathogenesis/disease process itself

## Treatment Philosophy: Chikitsa
Ayurvedic treatment is broadly divided into two categories:

### Shamana Chikitsa (Palliative/Pacifying Therapy)
Non-eliminative treatment aimed at balancing aggravated doshas in place, typically through herbs, diet, and lifestyle modification, without expelling toxins from the body.

### Shodhana Chikitsa (Purification/Panchakarma)
A more intensive detoxification protocol aimed at eliminating vitiated doshas and Ama from the body. This is the origin of **Panchakarma** ("five actions"):

1. **Vamana** (therapeutic emesis) — used primarily for Kapha-predominant conditions (e.g., respiratory congestion).
2. **Virechana** (therapeutic purgation) — used primarily for Pitta-predominant conditions.
3. **Basti** (medicated enema) — considered especially important for Vata disorders; described in classical texts as addressing a very wide range of conditions due to Vata's role in governing movement throughout the body.
4. **Nasya** (nasal administration of medicated oils/powders) — used for disorders of the head, neck, sinuses.
5. **Raktamokshana** (therapeutic bloodletting) — used historically for certain Pitta-blood-related skin conditions; among the least commonly practiced today due to modern safety standards.

Panchakarma is typically preceded by **Purvakarma** (preparatory procedures):
- **Snehana** — internal or external oleation (medicated oils/ghee)
- **Swedana** — therapeutic sudation/sweating (steam therapy)

And followed by **Paschatkarma** (post-procedure care), including a graduated return to normal diet (**Samsarjana Krama**).

## Other Common Therapeutic Procedures
- **Abhyanga** — full-body oil massage, used both preventively (as part of daily routine) and therapeutically.
- **Shirodhara** — continuous stream of warm medicated oil poured over the forehead, traditionally used for stress, insomnia, and certain neurological complaints.
- **Udvartana** — herbal powder massage, traditionally associated with Kapha reduction and improving circulation.
- **Pizhichil** — warm medicated oil poured over the body combined with massage, associated with Kerala-style Ayurveda, used for musculoskeletal and neurological conditions.
- **Kati/Greeva/Janu Basti** — localized oil pooling therapy over lower back, neck, or knee respectively, for joint/spinal conditions.
- **Netra Tarpana** — medicated ghee pooled over the eyes, traditionally for eye strain and certain ocular conditions.

## Rasayana (Rejuvenation Therapy)
A branch of Ayurveda focused on promoting longevity, immunity, and vitality (broadly analogous to, but distinct from, modern "anti-aging" or immunomodulatory concepts). Rasayana therapy may involve specific herbs, dietary regimens, and lifestyle practices intended to nourish Ojas and the dhatus.

## Ayurvedic Pharmacology Concepts (Dravyaguna)
Classical Ayurvedic pharmacology evaluates every substance (herb, mineral, food) along several axes:
- **Rasa** — taste (six recognized: sweet, sour, salty, pungent, bitter, astringent)
- **Guna** — inherent qualities (heavy/light, hot/cold, etc.)
- **Virya** — potency, generally categorized as heating or cooling
- **Vipaka** — post-digestive effect (the taste/effect a substance has after digestion, which may differ from its initial Rasa)
- **Prabhava** — a substance's unique, non-derivable special effect not explained by the above four factors

This framework is how classical texts assign herbs and formulations to dosha-balancing categories.


# Ayurveda Knowledge Base — Part 3: Lifestyle, Diet, and Materia Medica

## Dinacharya (Daily Routine)
Classical texts prescribe a daily regimen believed to maintain dosha balance:
- Waking before sunrise (Brahma Muhurta)
- Elimination and oral/tongue hygiene
- **Abhyanga** (self-oil massage) before bathing
- **Vyayama** (exercise) suited to one's constitution and season
- Meals at consistent times, largest meal at midday when digestive fire is considered strongest
- Avoiding daytime sleep (except in specific conditions)
- Winding down and sleeping at a consistent, relatively early hour

## Ritucharya (Seasonal Routine)
Ayurveda maps six seasons (Ritus) to changing dosha influences and adjusts diet/lifestyle accordingly:
- **Hemant/Shishira** (early/late winter) — Kapha accumulates; heavier, warming foods recommended.
- **Vasant** (spring) — accumulated Kapha liquefies; lighter, warming, detoxifying diet recommended.
- **Grishma** (summer) — Vata begins to accumulate; cooling, hydrating foods recommended.
- **Varsha** (monsoon) — digestive fire considered weak; light, warm, easily digestible food recommended, along with caution around Vata aggravation.
- **Sharad** (autumn) — Pitta accumulates; cooling, mildly sweet, bitter foods recommended.

## Ahara (Dietary Principles)
- **Shad Rasa (Six Tastes)**: sweet, sour, salty, pungent, bitter, astringent — a balanced meal is traditionally said to include all six, with proportions adjusted to individual constitution.
- **Dosha–Taste relationships** (general classical associations):
  - Sweet, sour, salty tastes tend to increase Kapha and decrease Vata.
  - Pungent, bitter, astringent tastes tend to increase Vata and decrease Kapha/Pitta (bitter and astringent reduce Pitta; pungent increases it).
  - Sour, salty, pungent tastes tend to increase Pitta.
- **Viruddha Ahara** (incompatible food combinations) — classical texts caution against certain combinations (e.g., milk with sour fruit, or heating and cooling foods together) as potentially disruptive to digestion; this concept has no direct one-to-one equivalent in modern nutrition science and should be presented as a traditional concept, not an established biochemical fact.
- Eating in a calm state, in moderation, and only when the previous meal is digested, are recurring classical recommendations.

## Materia Medica — Commonly Referenced Herbs
The following are widely documented in classical Ayurvedic pharmacology and modern ethnobotanical literature. Traditional uses are described here in general terms; none of this should be read as a dosage or treatment prescription — actual therapeutic use requires practitioner guidance and accounts for individual constitution, current health status, and interactions.

- **Ashwagandha** (*Withania somnifera*) — a Rasayana herb, traditionally used for vitality, stress resilience, and strength-building; classified with warming potency.
- **Turmeric / Haridra** (*Curcuma longa*) — widely used for its bitter-pungent taste and traditional association with skin health, wound healing, and as a general anti-inflammatory agent in classical texts.
- **Triphala** — a compound formulation of three fruits (Amalaki, Bibhitaki, Haritaki), one of the most widely referenced Ayurvedic formulations, traditionally used to support digestion and elimination and considered balancing for all three doshas.
- **Brahmi** (*Bacopa monnieri*) — traditionally associated with supporting memory and mental clarity (a "Medhya Rasayana," or cognitive-enhancing herb).
- **Guduchi / Giloy** (*Tinospora cordifolia*) — traditionally used to support immunity, described in classical texts as an anti-fever and general Rasayana herb.
- **Neem** (*Azadirachta indica*) — bitter, cooling; traditionally associated with skin health and Pitta pacification.
- **Tulsi / Holy Basil** (*Ocimum sanctum*) — considered sacred in Indian culture; traditionally used for respiratory support and as an adaptogen.
- **Amalaki / Amla** (*Phyllanthus emblica*) — a component of Triphala, high in vitamin C, traditionally considered a major Rasayana fruit.
- **Shatavari** (*Asparagus racemosus*) — traditionally associated with female reproductive health and as a general Rasayana.
- **Punarnava** (*Boerhavia diffusa*) — traditionally used in contexts related to fluid balance.
- **Guggul** (*Commiphora wightii*) — resin traditionally used in formulations associated with joint and metabolic support.

## Common Classical Formulations (Overview Only)
- **Chyawanprash** — a polyherbal jam-like formulation (Amalaki-based) traditionally taken as a general Rasayana/immune-supportive tonic.
- **Triphala Churna** — powdered form of the three-fruit combination described above.
- **Dashamoola** — a formulation of ten roots, used across various preparations (decoctions, oils) for Vata-related conditions.
- **Ashwagandharishta / Various Arishtas and Asavas** — fermented herbal liquid preparations, a distinct category of Ayurvedic formulation using self-generated alcohol as part of the extraction/preservation process.

## Classical Texts (Brihat Trayi — "The Great Three")
1. **Charaka Samhita** — foundational text of internal medicine (Kayachikitsa), attributed to Charaka; covers physiology, pathology, pharmacology, and treatment of internal diseases.
2. **Sushruta Samhita** — foundational text of surgery (Shalya Tantra), attributed to Sushruta; includes descriptions of surgical instruments, procedures, and is often cited as an early systematic surgical text.
3. **Ashtanga Hridaya** — compiled later by Vagbhata, synthesizing and organizing material from the two earlier texts; widely used as a teaching text due to its concise structure.

Additional referenced texts include the **Ashtanga Sangraha** (also by Vagbhata, a more detailed companion to Ashtanga Hridaya), **Madhava Nidana** (focused specifically on diagnosis/pathology), **Sharangdhara Samhita** (focused on pharmaceutics and formulation), and **Bhavaprakasha** (a later compendium including materia medica).

## Relationship to Yoga and Naturopathy
Ayurveda, Yoga, and Naturopathy are often grouped together (along with Unani, Siddha, Sowa-Rigpa, and Homeopathy under India's "AYUSH" umbrella) but are historically and theoretically distinct systems. Yoga's eight-limbed path (Ashtanga Yoga) is traditionally considered complementary to Ayurveda — both share root concepts from classical Indian philosophy (e.g., the three Gunas of mind) — but Yoga is not itself a subset of Ayurvedic medicine.



================================================================================
PART 1: THE CORE CONCEPTS & SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE
================================================================================

1. PANCHA MAHABHUTA (THE FIVE GREAT ELEMENTS)
Every physical entity is composed of five elemental principles:
- Akasha (Space/Ether): Non-resistance, porosity, expansiveness.
- Vayu (Air): Movement, kinetic energy, dryness, lightness.
- Teja / Agni (Fire): Transformation, heat, radiation, metabolic conversion.
- Jala / Apas (Water): Cohesion, fluidity, liquidity, lubrication.
- Prithvi (Earth): Solidity, mass, density, structural stability.

2. TRIDOSHA (THE THREE REGULATORY BIO-ENERGIES)
The elemental principles combine to govern physiological operations:

A. VATA (Space + Air)
- Primary Site: Colon (Pakwashaya), pelvis, thighs, bones, ears, skin.
- Qualities (Gunas): Dry (Ruksha), Light (Laghu), Cold (Sheeta), Rough (Khara), Minute (Sukshma), Mobile (Chala).
- Functional Roles: Neuromuscular conduction, cellular respiration, waste elimination, sensory reception.
- Sub-doshas:
  1. Prana Vata: Brain, chest; governs ingestion, respiration, sensory processing.
  2. Udana Vata: Throat, chest; governs speech, effort, memory, exhalation.
  3. Samana Vata: Stomach, small intestine; governs digestive movement and nutrient separation.
  4. Vyana Vata: Heart, circulatory system; governs cardiac rhythm, circulation, locomotion.
  5. Apana Vata: Pelvic floor, lower abdomen; governs urination, defecation, menstruation, ejaculation, parturition.

B. PITTA (Fire + Water)
- Primary Site: Small intestine (Grahani), stomach, liver, gall bladder, blood, eyes, skin.
- Qualities (Gunas): Oily (Sasneha), Sharp/Penetrating (Tikshna), Hot (Ushna), Light (Laghu), Odorous (Visra), Spreading (Sara), Liquid (Drava).
- Functional Roles: Enzymatic breakdown, thermoregulation, vision, intellectual discrimination (Dhi/Dhriti).
- Sub-doshas:
  1. Pachaka Pitta: Duodenum/stomach; primary digestive fire (Jatharagni).
  2. Ranjaka Pitta: Liver, spleen; governs hematopoiesis (erythrocyte pigment).
  3. Sadhaka Pitta: Heart; governs cognition, emotional processing, resolve.
  4. Alochaka Pitta: Eyes; visual perception and retinal function.
  5. Bhrajaka Pitta: Epidermis; cutaneous absorption, complexion, skin temperature.

C. KAPHA (Water + Earth)
- Primary Site: Chest (Uras), throat, head, joints, stomach, adipose tissue.
- Qualities (Gunas): Heavy (Guru), Cold (Sheeta), Soft (Mridu), Unctuous (Snigdha), Sweet (Madhura), Stable (Sthira), Slimy (Picchila).
- Functional Roles: Structural integrity, tissue cohesion, biological immunity (Ojas), fluid balance.
- Sub-doshas:
  1. Kledaka Kapha: Stomach lining; liquefies food, prevents mucosal autodigestion.
  2. Avalambaka Kapha: Thorax, heart, spine; provides physical support and lubrication to thoracic organs.
  3. Bodhaka Kapha: Oral cavity, tongue; taste perception and salivary enzymatic action.
  4. Tarpaka Kapha: Calvarium, cerebral sinuses; nourishes and buffers nervous tissue.
  5. Shleshaka Kapha: Synovial joints; lubricates articulations to prevent bone-on-bone friction.

3. SAPTA DHATU (THE SEVEN CONSTRUCTIVE TISSUES)
Tissues form in a sequential metabolic cascade:
1. Rasa: Plasma, chyle, interstitial fluid (Nourishment, hydration).
2. Rakta: Erythrocytes, oxygen-carrying components (Oxygenation, vitality).
3. Mamsa: Muscle tissue (Movement, structural support, covering).
4. Meda: Adipose tissue, subcutaneous fat (Lubrication, energy insulation).
5. Asthi: Bone, cartilage (Framework, protection of vital organs).
6. Majja: Bone marrow, neuro-tissue (Nerve conduction, filling skeletal voids).
7. Shukra / Artava: Gametes, reproductive fluids (Reproduction, vigor).
* End Product: OJAS (The biological essence of all seven Dhatus; responsible for autoimmune resilience and systemic homeostasis).

4. AGNI (THE METABOLIC FIRES)
Agni controls all physiological transformations. It comprises 13 distinct forms:
- 1 Jatharagni: The primary master digestive fire operating in the gastrointestinal tract.
- 5 Bhutagnis: Elemental transformational fires (Parthiva, Apya, Taijasa, Vayavya, Nabhasa) converting elemental matter into biological substrates.
- 7 Dhatvagnis: Micro-metabolic conversion fires operating within each of the 7 tissue levels.

States of Agni:
- Sama Agni: Balanced, optimal digestion and assimilation.
- Vishama Agni: Irregular, fluctuating digestion (associated with Vata disturbance).
- Tikshna Agni: Hyperactive, burning digestion (associated with Pitta disturbance).
- Manda Agni: Sluggish, hypoactive digestion (associated with Kapha disturbance).

5. AMA (ENDOGENOUS TOXIC BYPRODUCT)
When Agni is impaired (Manda or Vishama), food remains unassimilated. It undergoes fermentation and putrefaction in the gut, producing "Ama":
- Characteristics: Sticky, viscous, heavy, foul-smelling, occlusive.
- Effect: Blocks Srotas (micro- and macro-channels of circulation), inhibits immune responses, and acts as the nidus for inflammatory and autoimmune conditions.

================================================================================
PART 2: CLASSICAL LITERATURE & THE ASHTANGA DIVISIONS
================================================================================

1. THE BRIHAT TRAYI (THE THREE MAJOR CLASSICAL TEXTS)
- Charaka Samhita:
  Author: Acharya Charaka (based on Agnivesha Tantra).
  Focus: Internal Medicine (Kayachikitsa), etiology, clinical medicine, lifestyle regimens (Dinacharya/Ritucharya), medical ethics, philosophy of causation.
  Structure: 8 Sthanas (Sections) across 120 Chapters:
    * Sutra Sthana (Fundamental principles, pharmacology, diet)
    * Nidana Sthana (Pathology and disease etiology)
    * Vimana Sthana (Epidemiology, training of physicians, dosing)
    * Sharira Sthana (Anatomy, embryology, metaphysics)
    * Indriya Sthana (Prognosis and fatal signs)
    * Chikitsa Sthana (Therapeutics and disease treatment)
    * Kalpa Sthana (Pharmacy, preparation of emetics/purgatives)
    * Siddhi Sthana (Management of procedural complications in Panchakarma)

- Sushruta Samhita:
  Author: Acharya Sushruta.
  Focus: Surgical procedures (Shalya Tantra), surgical instruments (Yantras/Shastras), plastic reconstruction (rhinoplasty), burns, wound healing, anatomical dissection, Marma points (vital energy intersections).
  Structure: 6 Sthanas:
    * Sutra Sthana (Surgical rules, instruments, training)
    * Nidana Sthana (Surgical and systemic pathologies)
    * Sharira Sthana (Gross human anatomy, embryology, 107 Marma sites)
    * Chikitsa Sthana (Surgical operations, post-op care, rejuvenation)
    * Kalpa Sthana (Toxicology, snakebites, poisons and antidotes)
    * Uttara Tantra (Ophthalmology, ENT, head/neck, pediatrics, psychiatry)

- Ashtanga Hridaya & Ashtanga Sangraha:
  Author: Vagbhata.
  Focus: Concise, poetic synthesis harmonizing Charaka's internal medicine with Sushruta's surgical depth. Standardized clinical reference across India.

2. THE LAGHU TRAYI (THE THREE MINOR CLASSICAL TEXTS)
- Madhava Nidana (by Madhavakara): Specialized text purely on diagnosis, etiology, and differential pathology (Rogavinishchaya).
- Sharngadhara Samhita (by Sharngadhara): Details pharmaceutical processes, pulse diagnosis (Nadi Pariksha), preparation of herbal and mineral remedies.
- Bhavaprakasha (by Bhavamishra): Comprehensive materia medica (Dravyaguna), botanical properties, exotic imported herbs, nutritional science.

3. ASHTANGA AYURVEDA (THE EIGHT SPECIALIZED CLINICAL BRANCHES)
1. Kayachikitsa: General Internal Medicine (metabolic disorders, systemic fevers, arthritis, autoimmune conditions).
2. Shalya Tantra: General Surgery (excision, extraction of foreign bodies, incision of abscesses, wound suturing, fistula treatment via Kshara Sutra).
3. Shalakya Tantra: Ophthalmology, Otorhinolaryngology, Dentistry (supraclavicular medicine using fine probes and localized treatments).
4. Kaumarabhritya: Obstetrics, Gynecology, and Pediatrics (prenatal care, labor management, neonatal disorders, childhood illnesses).
5. Bhuta Vidya / Graha Chikitsa: Psychiatry, neuropsychiatry, idiopathic and microbial etiologies, psychosomatic disorders.
6. Agada Tantra: Clinical Toxicology (treatment of animal venoms, vegetable/mineral poisons, environmental toxins, cumulative biotoxins).
7. Rasayana Tantra: Geriatrics, Rejuvenation Medicine, Longevity (cellular revitalization, immune enhancement, memory restoration).
8. Vajikarana Tantra: Reproductive Medicine, Virility, and Eugenics (management of fertility, hormonal integrity, progeny health).

================================================================================
PART 3: PHARMACODYNAMICS, HERBS, AND DRUG DELIVERY SYSTEMS
================================================================================

1. RASA PANCHAKA (PHARMACOLOGICAL PARAMETERS)
Herbal and mineral actions are determined by 5 inherent attributes:

- Rasa (Initial Taste on Tongue):
  * Madhura (Sweet): Builds tissue, calms Vata & Pitta, increases Kapha.
  * Amla (Sour): Stimulates digestion, warms body, calms Vata, increases Pitta & Kapha.
  * Lavana (Salty): Retains moisture, softens masses, calms Vata, increases Pitta & Kapha.
  * Katu (Pungent): Dries fluid, clears sinuses, stimulates Agni, reduces Kapha, increases Vata & Pitta.
  * Tikta (Bitter): Cleanses tissues, anti-microbial, cooling, reduces Pitta & Kapha, increases Vata.
  * Kashaya (Astringent): Constricts vessels, dries secretions, heals wounds, reduces Pitta & Kapha, increases Vata.

- Guna (20 Fundamental Qualities):
  Heavy/Light, Cold/Hot, Unctuous/Dry, Slow/Sharp, Stable/Mobile, Soft/Hard, Clear/Slimy, Smooth/Rough, Minute/Gross, Viscous/Liquid.

- Virya (Active Potency):
  * Ushna (Hot potency): Accelerates metabolism, causes sweating, pacifies Vata/Kapha.
  * Sheeta (Cold potency): Soothing, tissue-toning, pacifies Pitta.

- Vipaka (Post-Digestive Metabolic Effect):
  * Madhura (Sweet Vipaka): Anabolic, increases Kapha, nourishes tissues.
  * Amla (Sour Vipaka): Mildly acidic, aggravates Pitta, promotes motility.
  * Katu (Pungent Vipaka): Catabolic, decreases Kapha, increases Vata.

- Prabhava (Specific Unique Action):
  Inherent action that transcends expected actions based on Rasa, Virya, or Vipaka.

2. FOUNDATIONAL MATERIA MEDICA (KEY MEDICINAL PLANTS)
- Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera):
  * Rasa: Tikta, Kashaya, Madhura | Virya: Ushna | Vipaka: Madhura.
  * Actions: Balya (strength-promoting), Rasayana, adaptogen, Vata-Kapha hara, nervous tonic.
- Amalaki (Phyllanthus emblica):
  * Rasa: Five tastes except salty (predominantly Amla/Sour) | Virya: Sheeta | Vipaka: Madhura.
  * Actions: Tridoshahara (balances all 3 doshas), potent antioxidant, rejuvenator, supports eyesight.
- Haridra / Turmeric (Curcuma longa):
  * Rasa: Tikta, Katu | Virya: Ushna | Vipaka: Katu.
  * Actions: Varnya (improves skin complexion), Krimighna (antimicrobial), Vishaghna (antitoxic), clears lymphatic channels.
- Guduchi (Tinospora cordifolia):
  * Rasa: Tikta, Kashaya | Virya: Ushna | Vipaka: Madhura.
  * Actions: Jvarahara (antipyretic), immunomodulator, cleanses Ama while toning Agni.
- Brahmi (Bacopa monnieri):
  * Rasa: Tikta, Kashaya, Madhura | Virya: Sheeta | Vipaka: Madhura.
  * Actions: Medhya (enhances intellect, memory, and cognitive processing), pacifies Pitta and Vata.
- Shatavari (Asparagus racemosus):
  * Rasa: Madhura, Tikta | Virya: Sheeta | Vipaka: Madhura.
  * Actions: Stanya (galactagogue), reproductive toner, soothing to inflamed mucosal membranes.

3. CLASSICAL FORMULATION CATEGORIES
- Churna: Micro-pulverized dried herbal powders (e.g., Triphala Churna, Trikatu Churna).
- Kashaya / Kwatha: Concentrated water decoctions made by boiling herbs down to 1/4th or 1/8th volume.
- Asava and Arishta: Naturally self-fermented hydroalcoholic liquid medicines (e.g., Ashwagandharishta, Draksharishta); shelf-stable, rapid cellular absorption.
- Ghrita: Medicated clarified butter preparations (e.g., Brahmi Ghrita, Mahatiktaka Ghrita); acts as a lipophilic carrier traversing the blood-brain barrier.
- Taila: Medicated oils (e.g., Mahanarayan Taila, Ksheerabala Taila) used for external transdermal absorption or internal administration.
- Bhasma: Purified, incinerated mineral/metallic calces (e.g., Swarna Bhasma, Shankha Bhasma); transformed into non-toxic, highly absorbable nanoparticles.

================================================================================
PART 4: MECHANISM OF DISEASE AND PURIFICATION PROTOCOLS
================================================================================

1. SHAT KRIYA KALA (THE SIX STAGES OF DISEASE PATHOGENESIS)
Diseases develop systematically through 6 sequential steps:
1. Sanchaya (Accumulation): A Dosha accumulates in its native anatomic seat due to dietary or seasonal triggers.
2. Prakopa (Aggravation): The accumulated Dosha becomes active, inflamed, or unstable in its original site.
3. Prasara (Overflow/Dissemination): The Dosha leaves its primary site and circulates systemically through bodily channels (Srotas).
4. Sthana-Samshraya (Localization/Nidus): The circulating Dosha settles in a anatomically weak site (Khavaigunya) or compromised tissue.
5. Vyakti (Symptom Manifestation): Clear, characteristic symptoms of the specific disease emerge in full.
6. Bheda (Complication/Chronicity): Structural, non-reversible cellular changes, tissue breakdown, or terminal complications arise.

2. PANCHAKARMA (THE FIVE PURIFICATORY PROCEDURES)
Panchakarma is designed to extract deep-seated systemic toxins (Ama) and excessive Doshas from tissues back into the GI tract for expulsion:

A. PURVA KARMA (PREPARATORY PROCEDURES - MANDATORY)
- Deepana & Pachana: Administering carminatives and digestives to burn off Ama and stabilize Agni.
- Snehana: Internal and external oleation (consuming increasing doses of medicated ghee and receiving full-body massage) to loosen toxins adhered to tissues.
- Svedana: Therapeutic sudation/steam therapies to dilate the Srotas, mobilizing liquefied toxins toward the digestive tract.

B. PRADHANA KARMA (THE FIVE PRIMARY DETOXIFICATION ACTIONS)
1. Vamana (Therapeutic Emesis):
   - Indication: Kapha-dominant conditions (bronchial asthma, psoriasis, chronic hyperacidity).
   - Route: Induced, controlled upper gastrointestinal evacuation.
2. Virechana (Therapeutic Purgation):
   - Indication: Pitta-dominant conditions (dermatitis, chronic liver congestion, inflammatory joint disorders).
   - Route: Induced, controlled lower gastrointestinal evacuation using herbal laxatives.
3. Basti (Medicated Enemas):
   - Indication: Vata-dominant conditions (sciatica, hemiplegia, constipation, parkinsonism, degenerative joint disease).
   - Types:
     * Niruha / Kashaya Basti: Cleansing herbal decoction-based enema.
     * Anuvasana / Sneha Basti: Nourishing lipid/oil-based retention enema.
4. Nasya (Transnasal Medication):
   - Indication: Supraclavicular pathology (sinusitis, chronic headaches, neurological/cranial conditions, cervical spondylosis).
   - Route: Administration of medicated drops, powders, or smokes through the nasal passages.
5. Raktamokshana (Therapeutic Blood Cleansing):
   - Indication: Severe systemic toxicity, localized blood-borne dermatological conditions, eczema, acute localized inflammation.
   - Methods: Jalaukavacharana (medicinal leech application), Siravyadha (venesection).

C. PASCHAT KARMA (POST-PROCEDURAL PROTOCOLS)
- Samsarjana Krama: A strict, step-by-step dietary progression starting with light liquid rice water (Manda), progressing to thin gruel (Peya), semi-solid gruel (Vilepi), and finally light solid meals (Yusha/Krita Yusha) over 3 to 7 days to rebuild the digestive fire safely.



In Ayurveda, food (Ahara) is considered the first pillar of health and the primary medicine. The core philosophy centers on maintaining digestive strength (Agni), balancing the three functional bio-energies (Doshas: Vata, Pitta, and Kapha), and preventing the accumulation of metabolic toxins (Ama).The Six Tastes (Shad Rasa)Ayurveda classifies all food using six fundamental tastes (Rasas). A balanced main meal ideally incorporates small amounts of all six, with proportions adjusted to balance an individual's dominant dosha.Taste (Rasa)Elemental CompositionEnergetic EffectImpact on DoshasCommon Food ExamplesMadhura (Sweet)Earth + WaterCooling, Heavy, NourishingDecreases Vata & Pitta; Increases KaphaWhole grains, milk, sweet fruits, root vegetablesAmla (Sour)Earth + FireHeating, Light, StimulatingDecreases Vata; Increases Pitta & KaphaLemons, yogurt, fermented foods, berriesLavana (Salty)Water + FireHeating, Heavy, LubricatingDecreases Vata; Increases Pitta & KaphaRock salt (Saindhava), sea salt, seaweedKatu (Pungent)Fire + AirHeating, Light, DryingDecreases Kapha; Increases Vata & PittaBlack pepper, chili, ginger, garlic, mustardTikta (Bitter)Air + SpaceCooling, Light, DetoxifyingDecreases Pitta & Kapha; Increases VataLeafy greens, turmeric, bitter melon, fenugreekKashaya (Astringent)Air + EarthCooling, Dry, ConstrictingDecreases Pitta & Kapha; Increases VataPomegranates, lentils, green tea, raw applesEating According to Body Type (Dosha)Ayurvedic dietetics uses the principle of opposites: if a quality is elevated in the body, foods with the opposite quality restore balance.Vata-Pacifying DietInherent Qualities: Cold, dry, light, irregular, mobile.Goal: Grounding, warming, nourishing, and hydrating.Tastes to Emphasize: Sweet, Sour, Salty.Tastes to Minimize: Bitter, Pungent, Astringent.Dietary Recommendations:Eat warm, cooked, freshly prepared meals (soups, stews, porridges).Use healthy fats liberally, such as ghee, sesame oil, and olive oil.Favor cooked root vegetables, well-soaked grains (oats, rice), and soaked nuts.Avoid raw salads, iced drinks, crackers, and dry, light snacks.Pitta-Pacifying DietInherent Qualities: Hot, sharp, oily, penetrating, light.Goal: Cooling, soothing, moderating, and mildly drying.Tastes to Emphasize: Sweet, Bitter, Astringent.Tastes to Minimize: Pungent, Sour, Salty.Dietary Recommendations:Favor cooling, mild, and non-acidic foods.Incorporate sweet fruits (melons, grapes, sweet apples), cucumber, coconut water, and leafy greens.Use cooling herbs and fats like coriander, fennel, cardamom, and ghee.Avoid heavily spiced foods, deep-fried items, vinegar, hard alcohol, and excessive caffeine.Kapha-Pacifying DietInherent Qualities: Heavy, cold, oily, slow, stable.Goal: Lightening, warming, stimulating, and drying.Tastes to Emphasize: Pungent, Bitter, Astringent.Tastes to Minimize: Sweet, Sour, Salty.Dietary Recommendations:Choose light, dry, warm, and highly spiced meals.Favor legumes, bitter greens, cabbage, broccoli, and light grains like barley, quinoa, and millet.Use warming spices generously: ginger, black pepper, cinnamon, and cumin.Minimize heavy dairy, refined sugars, deep-fried foods, and cold desserts.Classical Eating Guidelines (Ahara Vidhi)Beyond what is consumed, Ayurveda places equal weight on how meals are consumed to maintain proper enzymatic and metabolic function:Eat according to hunger: Consume meals only after the previous meal has fully digested (typically 3 to 5 hours apart).Eat the largest meal at midday: Digestive fire (Jatharagni) naturally peaks around noon when the sun is highest, mirroring circadian metabolic efficiency.Eat warm and freshly prepared food: Cold or stale food dampens digestive fire and creates systemic sluggishness.Avoid incompatible food combinations (Viruddha Ahara): Mixing foods with conflicting digestive requirements—such as dairy with sour fruits, or fish with milk—causes improper breakdown in the GI tract.Mindful consumption: Eat seated in a calm environment without high-stimulation distractions like screens or hurried walking.



 Ayurvedic Pathophysiology & Therapeutics Dataset
*Compiled for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Architecture*

## 1. Principles of Samprapti (Pathogenesis)
In Ayurveda, disease does not happen overnight. It follows the **Shat Kriya Kala** (Six Stages of Disease):
1. **Sanchaya (Accumulation):** Doshas build up in their natural home (e.g., Kapha in the stomach).
2. **Prakopa (Aggravation):** The localized doshas become highly reactive.
3. **Prasara (Dissemination):** The doshas overflow into the circulatory channels (Rasa/Rakta).
4. **Sthana Samshraya (Localization):** The circulating doshas find a weak point in the body (Khavaigunya) and lodge there.
5. **Vyakti (Manifestation):** Clear symptoms of the disease appear.
6. **Bheda (Chronicity/Complication):** Structural changes occur in the tissues; the disease becomes chronic or incurable.

## 2. Clinical Protocols for Major Diseases

### Disease: Amavata (Rheumatoid Arthritis / Inflammatory Joint Disease)
*   **Nidana (Etiology):** Consuming incompatible foods (Viruddha Ahara), exercising immediately after eating fatty foods, having poor digestive capacity (Mandagni).
*   **Samprapti (Pathophysiology):** Low digestive fire creates "Ama" (toxic, undigested metabolic sludge). Vata dosha pushes this Ama through the circulation, and it lodges in the Shleshaka Kapha (synovial fluid) of the joints.
*   **Lakshana (Symptoms):** Severe body ache, loss of appetite, swelling of joints, morning stiffness, fever.
*   **Chikitsa (Treatment Protocol):**
    *   *Langhana:* Fasting or light diet to digest the Ama.
    *   *Svedana:* Dry heat therapy (Valuka Sveda - sand bolus fomentation). **Crucial:** Wet/oil therapies are contraindicated initially because they increase Ama.
    *   *Aushadhi (Medicines):* Yogaraj Guggulu, Simhanada Guggulu, Rasna Erandadi Kwatha.
    *   *Shodhana:* Virechana (Purgation) to clear Pitta and Ama, followed by specialized Basti (Kshara Basti).

### Disease: Amlapitta (Hyperacidity / Peptic Ulcer Disease)
*   **Nidana (Etiology):** Excessive intake of sour, spicy, salty, and fermented foods; eating while stressed; staying awake late at night.
*   **Samprapti (Pathophysiology):** The Pitta dosha in the stomach becomes excessively liquid (Dravatva) and sour (Amlata). The normal digestive fire (Jatharagni) is smothered by this liquid Pitta, leading to improper digestion and mucosal burning.
*   **Lakshana (Symptoms):** Acid eructation (Khatta dakar), burning sensation in the chest and throat (Hritkantha daha), headache, nausea.
*   **Chikitsa (Treatment Protocol):**
    *   *Shodhana:* Vamana (Therapeutic emesis) if Kapha is dominant, or Virechana (Therapeutic purgation) if Pitta is dominant (standard).
    *   *Aushadhi (Medicines):* Cooling, bitter, and sweet herbs. Amalaki Churna, Shatavari Ghrita, Kamadudha Rasa (a mineral preparation containing calcium that acts as a potent antacid).
    *   *Pathya (Diet):* Barley, old rice, green gram, ash gourd, milk, and ghee. Avoid tomatoes, citrus, chilies, and yogurt.

### Disease: Prameha / Madhumeha (Metabolic Syndrome / Type II Diabetes)
*   **Nidana (Etiology):** Sedentary lifestyle (Asyasukh), excessive sleep, heavy consumption of dairy, jaggery, newly harvested grains, and marshy meats.
*   **Samprapti (Pathophysiology):** Kapha dosha and Meda (adipose tissue) increase and block the channels of fluid metabolism. Eventually, Vata gets provoked and pushes the vital essence of the body (Ojas) out through the urine, leading to Madhumeha (sweet urine).
*   **Lakshana (Symptoms):** Turbid and copious urination (Prabhuta Avila Mutrata), excessive thirst, burning in palms and soles, lethargy.
*   **Chikitsa (Treatment Protocol):**
    *   *Categorization:* Overweight patients receive "Apatarpana" (depletion/detoxification therapies). Thin, frail patients receive "Santarpana" (nourishing therapies).
    *   *Aushadhi (Medicines):* Haridra (Turmeric) with Amalaki (Nisha-Amalaki combo is standard), Shilajit, Chandraprabha Vati, Vasant Kusumakar Rasa (for neuropathic complications).
    *   *Vyayama:* Rigorous physical exercise is mandated.

## 3. Mechanisms of Drug Delivery (Kalpana)
Ayurveda designs formulations specifically for targeted absorption:
*   **Ghrita (Medicated Ghee):** Used for neurological and psychological disorders (Unmada, Apasmara) because the lipophilic nature of ghee crosses the blood-brain barrier effectively.
*   **Asava/Arishta (Fermented Decoctions):** Used for low digestion (Mandagni) and structural weaknesses. The alcohol ensures rapid gastric absorption and serves as a vasodilator, delivering active alkaloids quickly.
*   **Bhasma (Nano-calcined minerals):** Used in severe or chronic diseases. The incineration processes reduce metals (like gold, copper, iron) to non-toxic nanoparticles that act as catalysts at the cellular level.

LEGAL NOTICES 

In India there are laws dealing with drugs that are the subject of monographs which 
follow. These monographs should be read subject to the restrictions imposed by these laws 
wherever they are applicable. 

It is expedient that enquiry be made in each case in order to ensure that the provisions of 
the law are being complied with. 

In general, the Drugs & Cosmetics Act, 1940 (subsequently amended in 1964 and 
1982), the Dangerous Drugs Act, 1930 and the Poisons Act, 1919 and the rules framed 
thereunder should be consulted. 

Under the Drugs & Cosmetics Act, the Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of India (A.P.I.), Part- 
II, Vol. I, is the book of standards for compound formulations included therein and the 
standards prescribed in the Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of India, Part-I, (Formulation) Vol. I, 
would be official. If considered necessary these standards can be amended and the Chairman of 
the Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia Committee s authorised to issue such amendements. Whenever 
such amendments are issued the Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of India, Part-II (Formulation), Vol. 
I, would be deemed to have been amended accordingly. 

 

GENERAL NOTICES 

Title : The title of the book is Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of India, Part-II (Formulations) 
Volume-I. Wherever the abbreviation API, Pt.-II,Vol.-I is used, it may be presumed to stand 
for the same and the supplements or amendments thereto. 

Name of the Formulation: The name given on top of each monograph is in Samskrt, as 
mentioned in the Ayurvedic Formulary of India (AFI) and will be considered official. These 
names have been arranged in English alphabetical order under each category of dosage form. 

Ingredients and Processes: Formulations are prepared from individual ingredients that comply 
with the requirements for those individual ingredients for which monographs are provided in 
the volumes of API, Part-I. Where water is used as an ingredient it should meet the 
requirements for Potable Water covered by its monograph in the Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of 
India-Part-I. 

Monograph for each formulation includes the full composition together with directions 
for its preparation. Such composition and directions are intended for preparation of small 
quantities for short-term supply and use. When so prepared, no deviation from the stated 
composition and directions is permitted. However, if such a preparation is manufactured on a 
large scale with the intention of sale or distribution, deviations from the directions given are 
permitted, but maintaining the same ratio as stated in the monographs with the ingredients 
complying with the compendial requirements, and also that the final product meets the 
following criteria: 

(a) complies with all of the requirements stated in the monograph on compound formulations, 

(b) in the composition of certain formulations it has been allowed that a specified part of the 
plant may be substituted by another part of the same plant. In such cases the manufacturer 
should mention on the label the actual part of the plant used in the formulation. 

(c) wherever an official substitute is provided for, deviation from the original formulation is 
permitted, using the official substitute . 

(d) wherever a formulation composition specifies a drug that is banned from commerce, this 
may be omitted, and the fact mentioned on the label. 

If a preparation is intended to be stored over a period of time, deterioration due to 
microbial contamination may be inhibited by the addition to the formula of a permitted 
preservative. In such circumstances the label should state the concentration of the preservative 
and the appropriate storage conditions. It is implied that such a preparation will be effectively 
preserved according to the appropriate criteria applied. 

The direction that an ingredient in a formulation must be freshly prepared indicates that 
it must be prepared and used within 24 hours. 

Monograph: Each monograph begins with a definition and introductory paragraph indicating 

the formulation composition, scientific names of the drugs used with their botanical parts along 
with a brief account of the method of preparation. 

 

The requirements given in the monographs are not framed to provide against all 
impurities, contaminants or adulterants; they provide appropriate limits only for possible 
impurities that may be permitted to a certain extent. Material found to contain an impurity, 
contaminant or adulterant which is not detectable by means of the prescribed tests are also to be 
considered as impurity should rational consideration require its absence. 

Standards: For statutory purposes, the following shall be considered official standards: 
Definition, Formulation composition, Identification, Physico-chemical parameters, Assay and 
Other requirements. 

Added Substances: A formulation contains no added substances except when specifically 
permitted in the individual monograph. Unless otherwise specified in the individual 
monograph, or elsewhere in the General Notices, suitable substances may be added from the 
approved list of Drugs and Cosmetics Rules, under Rule 169 to a formulation to enhance its 
stability, usefulness, elegance, or to facilitate its preparation. Such auxiliary substances shall 
be harmless in the amounts used, shall not exceed the minimum quantity required to provide 
their intended effect, shall not impair the therapeutic efficacy or the bioavailability and safety of 
the preparation and shall not interfere with the tests and assays prescribed for determining 
compliance with the official standards. Particular care should be taken to ensure that such 
substances are free from harmful organisms. Though the manufacturer of a formulation is given 
the freedom to use an added substance, the manufacturer must guarantee the innocuousness of 
the added substance. The manufacturer shall also be responsible to explain to the appropriate 
authority, if needed, regarding the purpose of the added substance(s). 

### Description Statement given under this title is not to be interpreted in a strict sense although 
they may help in the evaluation of an article. However substantial departure form the 
requirement will not be acceptable. 

Capital Letters in the Text: The names of the Pharmacopoeial substances, preparations and 
other materials in the text are printed in capital initial letters, and these infer that materials of 
Pharmacopoeial quality have been used. 

Italics: Italic types are used for Scientific names of the plant drugs and microorganisms, and for 
some sub-headings and certain notations of the chemical names. Italic types have also been 
used for words which refer to solvent system in TLC procedure, reagents and substances, 
processes covered under Appendices. Chemicals and Reagents and Substances of Processes in 
Appendices have also been printed in Italics. 

Odour and Taste: Wherever a specific odour has been observed it has been mentioned as 
characteristic for that formulation, but the description as odourless or no odour has generally 
been avoided in the Description where a substance has no odour. Where a characteristic odour 
is said to be present it is examined by smelling the drug directly after opening the container. If 
such an odour is discernible, the contents are rapidly transferred to an open vessel and re- 
examined after 15 minutes. If odour persists to be discernible, the sample complies with the 
description for odour, characteristic for that formulation. 

 

The taste of a drug is examined by taking a small quantity of drug by the tip of moist 
glass rod and allowing it on tongue previously moistened with water. This does not apply in the 
case of poisonous drugs. 

Powder fineness: Wherever the powder of a drug is required, it shall comply with the mesh 
number indicated in the Monograph. 

Where particle size is prescribed in a Monographs, the specified sieve number are used to 
fractionate a weighed representative sample from the container, each fraction weighed 
separately, and expressed as a percentage of the weight taken initially, to obtain compliance 
with the monograph. 

Weights and Measures: The metric system of weights and measures is employed. Weights are 
given in multiples or fractions of a gram (g) or of a milligram (mg). Fluid measures are given in 
multiples of fraction of milliliter (ml). The amount stated is approximate but the quantity 
actually used must be accurately weighed and must not deviate by more than 10 per cent from 
the one stated. 

When the term drop is used measurement is to be made by means of a tube which 
delivers 20 drops per gram of distilled water at 15 . 

Identity, Purity and Strength: Under the heading Identification , tests are provided as an aid 
to identification and are described in the respective monographs. Microscopical characters are 
prescribed for the individual ingredients where these do not exceed ten in number, added in 
situ . Appendix 2.1 gives detailed procedure 

Vegetable drugs used in formulations, should be duly identified and authenticated and 
be free from insects, pests, fungi, micro organisms, pesticides, and other animal matter 
including animal excreta, be within the permitted and specified limits for lead, arsenic and 
heavy metals, and show no abnormal odour, colour, sliminess, mould or any sign of 
deterioration. 

The quantitative tests like total ash, acid-insoluble ash, water-soluble ash, alcohol- 
soluble extractive, water-soluble extractive, moisture content, volatile oil content and assays are 
the parameters upon which the standards of Pharmacopoeia depend. Except for Assays, which 
are covered under each monograph, the methods of determination for others are given in 
Appendices, with a suitable reference to the specific appendix. 

The analyst is not precluded from employing an alternate method in any instance if he is 
satisfied that the method, which he uses will give the same result as the Pharmacopoeial method 
described under assay. However, in the event of doubt or dispute the methods of analysis of the 
Pharmacopoeia are alone authoritative. Unless otherwise prescribed, the assays and tests are 
carried out at a temperature between 20 and 30 . 

In the performance of assay or test procedures, not less than the specified number of 
dosage units should be taken for analysis. Proportionately larger or smaller quantities than the 
specified weights and volumes of assay or test substances and Reference Standards or Standard 
Preparations may be taken, provided the measurement is made with at least equivalent accuracy 

 

and provided that any subsequent steps, such as dilutions, are adjusted accordingly to yield 
concentrations equivalent to those specified and are made in such manner as to provide at least 
equivalent accuracy. 

Where it is directed in the assay for Tablet formulation to weigh and powder not less 
than a given number, usually 20, of the tablets, it is intended that a counted number of tablets 
shall be weighed and reduced to a fine powder. Likewise, where it is directed in the assay for 
Capsules to remove, as completely as possible, the contents of not less than a given number, 
usually 20, of the capsules, it is intended that a counted number of capsules should be carefully 
opened and the contents quantitatively removed, combined, mixed, and weighed accurately. 
The portion of the powdered tablets or the mixed contents of the capsules taken for assay is 
representative of the whole tablets or capsules, respectively, and is, in turn, weighed accurately. 
The result of the assay is then related to the amount of active ingredients per tablet in the case 
of tablets and per capsule in the case of capsules from the weight of contents of each 
tablet/capsule. 

Limits for Heavy metals, Microbial load, Pesticide residues and Aflatoxins : Formulations 
included in this volume are required to comply with the limits for heavy metals, microbial load, 
pesticide residues and aflatoxins prescribed in individual monographs and wherever limit is not 
given they must comply with the limits given in Appendix. The methods for determination of 
these parameters are given in Appendices. 

Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC): Under this title, wherever given, the R values given in 
the monographs are not absolute but only indicative. The analyst may use any other solvent 
system and detecting reagent to establish the identity of any particular chemical constituent 
reported to be present in the formulation. However in case of dispute the pharmacopoeial 
method would prevail. Unless specified in the individual monograph all TLC have been carried 
out on pre-coated Silica gelG F254 aluminium plates. 

Reference Standards: Reference substance and standard preparation are authentic substances 
that have been verified for there suitability for use as standards for comparison in some assays, 
tests and TLC of the API. 

Constant Weight: The term constant weight when it refers to drying or ignition means that 
two consecutive weighing do not differ by more than 1.0 mg per gram of the substance taken 
for the determination, the second weighing following an additional hour of drying or further 
ignition. 

Percentage of Solutions In defining standards, the expression per cent (%), is used, 
according to circumstances, with one of the four meanings given below. 

Per cent w/w (percentage weight in weight) expresses the number of grams of active substance 
in 100 grams of product. 

Per cent w/v (percentage weight in volume) expresses the number of grams of active substance 
in 100 milliliters of product. 

Per cent v/v (percentage volume in volume) expresses the number of milliliters of active 
substance in 100 milliliters of product. 

 

Per cent v/w (percentage volume in weight) expresses the number of milliliters of active 
substance in 100 grams of product. 

Percentage of Alcohol: All statements of percentage of alcohol (C2H5OH) refer to percentage 
by volumes at 15.56 c. 

Temperature: Unless otherwise specified all temperatures refer to centigrade (Celsius), 
thermometric scale and all measurement are made at 25 . 

Solutions: Unless otherwise specified in the individual monograph, all solutions are prepared 
with Purified Water. 

Reagents and Solutions: Reagents required for the assay and tests of the Pharmacopoeia are 
defined in the Appendix showing the nature, degree of the purity and strength of solutions to be 

made from them. 

Filtration: Where it is directed to filter, without further qualification, it is intended that the 
liquid be filtered through suitable filter paper or equivalent device until the filtrate is clear. 

Soluble substances: The following table indicates the meaning of degree of solubilities: 

Descriptive Terms Relative quantities of solvent 
Very soluble less than 1 part 

Freely soluble from 1 to 10 parts 

Soluble from 10 to 30 parts 

Sparingly soluble from 30 to 100 parts 

Slightly soluble from 100 to 1000 parts 

Very slightly soluble from 1000 to 10,000 parts 
Practically insoluble more than 10,000 parts 

The term partly soluble is used to describe a mixture of which only some of the components 
dissolve. 

### Therapeutic Uses Therapeutic uses of the formulations mentioned in this Pharmacopoeia are 
as given in the Ayurvedic Formulary of India. 

Doses: The doses mentioned in each monograph are in metric system which are the 
approximate conversions from classical weights mentioned in Ayurvedic texts. A conversion 
table is appended giving classical weights with their metric equivalents.(Appendix 7) Doses 
mentioned in the Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of India (API) are intended merely for general 
guidance and represent, unless otherwise stated, the average range of quantities per dose which 
is generally regarded suitable by clinicians for adults only when administered orally. They are 
not to be regarded as binding upon the prescribers. 

 

The medical practitioner will exercise his own judgment and act on his own 

responsibility in respect of the amount of the formulation he may prescribe or administer or on 
the frequency of its administration. If it is usual to administer a medicine by a method other 
than by mouth, the single dose suitable for that method of administration is mentioned. 
Storage: Statement under the heading Storage constitutes non-mandatory advice. The 
substances and preparations of the Pharmacopoeia are to be stored under conditions that prevent 
contamination and, as far as possible, deterioration. Precautions that should be taken in relation 
to the effects of the atmosphere, moisture, heat and light are indicated, where appropriate, in the 
individual monographs. 

Specific directions are given in some monographs with respect to the temperatures at 
which Pharmacopoeial articles should be stored, where it is considered that storage at a lower or 
higher temperature may produce undesirable results. The conditions are defined by the 
following terms. 

Cold- Any temperature not exceeding 8 and usually between 2 and 8 . A refrigerator is cold 
place in which the temperature is maintained thermostatically between 2 and 8 . 

Cool- Any temperature between 8 and 25 . An article for which storage in a cool place is 
directed may, alternately, be stored in a refrigerator, unless otherwise specified in the individual 
monograph. 

Room temperature-The temperature prevailing in a working area. 

Warm- Any temperature between 30 and 40 . 

Excessive heat- Any temperature above 40 . 

Protection from freezing- Where, in addition to the risk of breaking of the container, freezing 
results in loss of strength or potency or in destructive alteration of the characteristics of an 
article the label on the container bears an appropriate instruction to protect from freezing. 
Storage under non-specific conditions- Where no specific storage directions or limitations are 
given in the individual monograph, it is to be understood that the storage conditions include 

protection from moisture, freezing and excessive heat. 

Containers: The container is the device that holds the article. The immediate container is that 
which is in direct contact with the article at all times. The closure is a part of the container. 

The container is designed so that the contents may be taken out for the indented purpose in a 
convenient manner. It provides the required degree of protection to the contents from the 

environmental hazards. 

The container should not interact physically or chemically with the article placed in it so as to 
alter the strength, quality or purity of the article beyond the official requirements. 

 

Prior to its being filled, the container should be clean. Special precautions and cleaning 
procedures may be necessary to ensure that each container is clean and that extraneous matter is 
not introduced into or onto the article. 

Light-resistant Container- A light resistant container protects the contents from the effects of 
actinic light by virtue of the specific properties of the material of which it is made. 
Alternatively, a clear and colourless or a translucent container may be made light-resistant by 
means of an opaque (light-resistant) covering and/or stored in a dark place: in such cases, the 
label on the container should bear a statement that the opaque covering or storage in dark place 
is needed until the contents have been used up. 

Well-closed Container- A well-closed container protects the contents from extraneous solids 
and liquids and from loss of the article under normal conditions of handling, shipment, storage 
and distribution. 

Tightly-closed Container- A tightly-closed container protects the contents form contamination 
by extraneous liquids solids or vapours, from loss or deterioration of the article from 
effervescence, deliquescence or evaporation under normal conditions of handling, shipment, 
storage and distribution. 

Single Unit Container- A single unit container is one that is designed to hold a quantity of the 
drug product intended for administration as a single finished device intended for use promptly 
after the container is opened. The immediate container and/or outer container or protective 
packaging is so designed as to show evidence of any tampering with the contents. 

Multiple Unit Container- A multiple unit container is container that permits withdrawals of 
successive portions of the contents without changing the strength, quality or purity of the 

remaining portion. 

Tamper-evident Container- A tamper-evident container is fitted with a device or mechanism 
that reveals irreversibly whether the container has been opened. 

Labelling: In general, the labeling of drugs and pharmaceuticals is governed by the Drugs and 
Cosmetics Act, 1940 and Rules there under.
